The Atlantic
What a Bicentennial Baby Knows
2026-08-24

What a Bicentennial Baby Knows

The Atlantic | 2026-08-24

Visit Original Website
Thirteen seconds after midnight on July 4, 1976, Karen Steele was born in Montclair, New Jersey, and was anointed the first “bicentennial baby.” Newspaper articles followed. President Gerald Ford and his wife, Betty, sent a note to the family. And then it kind of became a thing. Any baby born that year was treated like they had emerged playing the fife and drums. In Mobile, Alabama, new mothers received a commemorative blanket. In Covington, Louisiana, a hospital handed out (for $2.50) special “bicentennial baby” certificates on faux parchment, a “memento that can be framed and cherished as a family heirloom for endless generations.” This was a year of jaw-dropping kitsch, an explosion of red, white, and blue everything—toilet paper, lollipops, even condoms (pleading: One Time for Old Glory). Branding newborns this way became part of the general patriotic fun. And although the tchotchkes surely ended up moldering in a red, white, and blue landfill somewhere, the label stuck. I know: I was one of those babies, born in August 1976. Being a bicentennial baby was not anything I ever thought about all that much. In fact, it was really only when meeting another person born in 1976 that it came up at all. Oh, you’re a bicentennial baby too, one of us would say to the other, smiling dumbly. This was a secret handshake that opened zero doors. But unlike other indelible parts of my identity that felt small in their particularity—like being the child of immigrants or being nearsighted—having my birth hitched somehow to the history of the country, to its forward motion, felt big whenever I was reminded of it. Beyond the horror of realizing that five of my current lifespans stacked up is all that separates me now from Benjamin Franklin, I’ve been thinking about this cohort of which I’m a part. Expounding on the significance of one’s own generation can lead rapidly to wild generalization, self-absorption, and some misplaced nostalgia. I know this. But I’m convinced that my fellow bicentennial babies and I have been through something unique. We’ve followed a distinct arc over the past 50 years: one of disillusionment. Better than most, we understand the disjunctions of our present because they happened at crucial inflection points alongside the timeline of our lives. We were born during a period of palate-cleansing. The Vietnam War was over. President Richard Nixon had, in 1974, resigned after Watergate, a redemptive scandal that renewed people’s faith in self-government. Journalists—yes, journalists—were sex symbols. Robert Redford played Bob Woodward in All the President’s Men. And the peanut farmer Jimmy Carter, a man who campaigned teeth-first, would be elected president in the fall, having promised a new era of idealism. Read: Inside America’s ugly birthday battle I don’t remember any of this, of course, but babies like me were launched into an atmosphere of reawakened good vibes. When Time magazine commissioned the polling firm Yankelovich, Skelly & White to gauge the country’s temperature from June to September of 1976, the company found a 10 percent jump in positivity on its “state of the nation index” between those months, the most rapid increase it had seen since it began tracking the metric. The following years were also a time of crime and stagflation and the Iran hostage crisis, but at least by the indicators used by pollsters to determine collective mood, things continued to get generally sunnier into the ’80s. “The Zeitgeist has turned zesty,” Kurt Andersen wrote in a 1984 assessment of that year’s numbers. “The U.S. is at peace, and between rising employment and fading inflation, the economy is aglow. Americans are feeling more sanguine and comfortable about their country than they have felt in two decades. A rebirth of the American spirit, as Carter dearly hoped five summers ago? It sure feels like it.” I remember this optimism and confidence. On November 9, 1989, I sat watching television with my grandfather—a Holocaust survivor who that day, and most days, had his shirt off and a captain’s hat on his head. The Berlin Wall was coming down. This happened when we bicentennial babies were 13 and highly impressionable. Look at them, my grandfather said, his voice breaking, as people pick-axed their way out of East Germany. I recall the total elation on their faces when they finally broke through, and then the bracing thought that they were trying to get to the side where I already lived. For many young Americans in those years, a feeling of transcendence was possible. You had a sense that good fortune had placed you somewhere that everyone in the world wished they could be. At the same time, that wider world, with its crumbling empires and ethnic cleansing and genocides, seemed very far away. Even the Gulf War of the early ’90s appeared, to those of us safe at home, as a ghostly green video game playing on CNN, transmitted from somewhere hard to recognize as real. You may not believe me when I tell you that I read Francis Fukuyama’s The End of History and the Last Man at 19, for fun, when I was a junior and living abroad in France. But I did, and then after college I went into the Peace Corps and lived in a village in Cameroon for two years. Although many of the people there thought that I was working for the CIA (I was not), I felt I was doing something uncomplicatedly wholesome, even a little patriotic. We were 25 when 9/11 happened. I moved to New York City in October of that year, like a salmon swimming upstream—or like a salmon following a girl. Everywhere were posters of missing people. During my first days of couch surfing, New Yorkers were further terrorized by anthrax spores sent through the mail, addressed to political and media targets. That terrible fall was a hinge moment for all Americans but maybe especially for us young adults, who were also at a hinge moment in our lives, suddenly vulnerable and less innocent in new ways, feeling silly for having believed that history could have an end. Jerusalem Demsas: The end of Francis Fukuyama I haven’t mentioned the internet yet, but it also came around for us as adulthood hit. I got my first email address in college and accessed my inbox in the library basement at a computer lab that perpetually smelled like Doritos. It was all so new that you could forget to send a paper to a professor and then offer the excuse that the email probably just got lost. I spent a lot of time outside that library, chatting, with nothing in my hands besides a cigarette. I think I knew someone who had a beeper. We got flip phones in our 20s. We were in our early 30s when the first iPhone arrived and Facebook showed up. We were told that these were revolutionary tools. Maybe we believed this more than other people did, still being relatively young and feeling like we were being handed powerful megaphones in our prime—a chance to make change, to connect. Then the tools congealed into yet more products that would waste our hours and make us feel terrible about ourselves and our fellow citizens. We made lives against the backdrop of distant wars, unceasing notifications popping up on our phones. The year we turned 40, Donald Trump was elected president. Four years later came COVID. Just as we were balancing more and more—perhaps children, a challenging job, ailing parents—the world seemed to conspire to keep our heads spinning. In these past few years, before we and the country hit our big milestones, we were assaulted by panicked warnings about the state of American democracy—and the future of the entire human race. What has AI been up to over the past few days? Creating new viruses and independently cyberhacking, it appears. We are less naive, though more exhausted. It has not been lost on us that the people warning that AI could lead to our extinction are the same people who invented it. This is a pattern we’re familiar with now: Technology gives us something with one hand while robbing us of something else with the other. I’m leaving out a lot. We also had private joys and tragedies totally impervious to what America writ large was up to. But I can distinguish a progression toward an increased sense of emergency, toward alienation, toward a mournfulness that makes me read the newspaper through my fingers most days. I don’t think this is the birthday blues talking—or, if I’m honest, maybe not just the birthday blues. It’s also the metrics: about loneliness and economic stagnation and political polarization and the whole insane technological surround sound of our lives. My point is that bicentennial babies, regardless of their individual circumstances, have all been strapped into the first row of this ride. Someone born on America’s centennial, in 1876, could well have argued that they had witnessed much more consequential change by their 50th birthday. They might have gushed about the expansion of railroads and telegraphs, about seeing the first airplanes, telephones, and radios, and then explained what it was like to face the reality that as many as 20 million people could be killed in a single war. I wouldn’t want to go head-to-head with them. Every generation has its story. But we bicentennial babies have lived through a major rupture. For all else that might be good in our lives—and I’m lucky to have a lot that’s good—this is not such an exuberant place to find ourselves at 50. Read: Trump’s 250th celebration is a fiasco Can we imagine anyone proudly calling the babies born this year—what? semiquincentennial sprouts? A mouthful, granted. But that’s not what makes it sound strange. Behind the “bicentennial babies” moniker was a kind of hope. We would be the Americans of the future. And we would be moving toward that future not with dread but with anticipation. Whatever one’s political affiliation, people do not have much enthusiasm these days when they picture where we’re headed. When the Pew Research Center surveyed Americans just before this year’s semiquincentennial, 59 percent of respondents said that the country’s best years were behind it. Asked to imagine the United States 50 years from now, 44 percent were pessimistic and only 28 percent were optimistic. Perhaps most telling, 27 percent chose neither option; they just felt blah. Recently, Conan O’Brian, speaking on the podcast The Rest Is History, talked about the “undiluted joy” he remembers feeling as a preteen experiencing the bicentennial. Now: “It’s very hard for some people, maybe, to just say, I’m really excited about our 250th when our current state is so divided.” I can’t conceive of myself being around for the tricentennial—and if I did suggest living to 100, my mother might faint from all the spitting she’d have to do to ward off the evil eye. But if I do make it there, I will have lived through a third of the country’s existence. I’ll have a lot more data to work with. Will 2026 appear in retrospect as another hinge, or simply as a snapshot of us—mouths open and eyes closed, as in an amusement-park photograph—mid–free fall? Will there be self-correction or a piling-on of bad decisions? Disintegration or newfound connection? No one can really tell. Though I do know what I would like on my 50th birthday and the country’s 250th: something for us to look forward to. ​​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. The Trump Comedians Are Tired
2026-08-24

The Trump Comedians Are Tired

The Atlantic | 2026-08-24

Visit Original Website
In 2017, as Donald Trump was getting used to the White House furniture, Alec Baldwin was feeling exultant. His impersonation of the new president on Saturday Night Live had made him momentarily inescapable and given the comedy show its highest ratings in years. To capitalize on the momentum, Baldwin co-authored a book-length satirical memoir written from Trump’s perspective. Baldwin’s autobiography—which also came out that year and was excerpted as a cover story for Vanity Fair—noted that his take on the president “seemed to delight nearly everyone in the People’s Republic of Manhattan.” When he accepted an Emmy for his performance in the fall, he addressed Trump directly: “At long last, Mr. President, here is your Emmy.” It was disorienting, then, to hear Baldwin declare, when I spoke with him earlier this summer, that this sort of comedy has become stale, boring, and useless. “None of it’s funny. I don’t find it funny anymore,” he told me. “There’s nothing left to say about Trump.” Clearly, something has changed—and not just for Baldwin, who effectively retired from the role back in 2020. The first Trump administration was a boon for comedians and for television. A cottage industry of new, politically inflected clip shows offered satirical commentary on the new president to a mostly liberal audience. Late-night comedy adopted Jon Stewart’s old playbook, trading quippy gags for more full-throated (and sometimes outright defiant) critiques of government. Ambiguity was out; everyone knew whose side these commentators were on, and other impersonators began vying for Baldwin’s crown. But the bubble collapsed right around the moment of Joe Biden’s ascension, and Trump’s return to power hasn’t inspired the same volume of riffs. Today, some of the comedians who rode the initial wave have come to feel a little like Baldwin does. They’re sick of the shtick—and they’re skeptical about the function of political comedy in a world that’s arguably beyond parody. Read: Why South Park did an about-face on mocking Trump Part of what once made Trump such a rich vein for comedic send-ups was that, at the start of his political career, he seemed straightforwardly ridiculous. Hadn’t this guy been shilling for Omaha Steaks with Gary Busey and Lil Jon? How would he spin the Oreo commercial he’d done with Peyton and Eli Manning, in which he’d challenged the brothers to a “lick race”? The jokes practically wrote themselves. Anthony Atamanuik, a Trump impersonator whose parody series, The President Show, ran for one season on Comedy Central in 2017, told me that at its best, the political comedy of that era also had a kind of diagnostic power: “I wanted my show to continue mostly because I saw each episode as an essay on an issue in American life, and the albatross I had was Trump.” His hope was that, for a liberal audience still reeling from the shock of his election, skewering the president could be a way to crystallize what had happened to the country. That initial burst of left-leaning satire didn’t last. Studios gave the former Daily Show correspondents Michelle Wolf, Larry Wilmore, Hasan Minhaj, and Wyatt Cenac their own shows, only to cancel them before the end of the president’s first term. A 2020 New York Times Magazine story on this “boom and bust” suggested that some comedians—and some audiences—were tiring of the material even before it became clear that Trump would lose the election. Network late-night shows continued to treat the president as a punching bag, but the novelty had worn off. Overexposure is one explanation for that decline. Another is that these liberal viewers started to expect something different from comedy as the reality of MAGA’s ascendance set in. More than over-the-top impersonators and Daily Show correspondents ridiculing the news, people seemed to be craving simple palliatives. By 2020, the most visible Trump satirist was the comedian Sarah Cooper, who filmed herself mouthing along to the president’s public statements while mimicking his facial expressions. Her work was almost post-comedic: Rather than writing her own material, she was giving people permission to laugh at the strangeness of his mannerisms. She eventually parlayed viral fame into a guest-hosting gig on Jimmy Kimmel Live and a Netflix special directed by Natasha Lyonne. Trump’s return to office in 2025 could have been an opportunity for any of these comedians to stage comebacks, or for new shows to emerge. But although the president remains a target in liberal comedy circles, the market for this brand of humor has shrunk considerably. It remains most evident in today’s late-night lineup: The Daily Show, one of the progenitors of the form, remains on the air. (Its original host, Jon Stewart, returned in 2024.) Last Week Tonight With John Oliver—which once cautioned viewers against voting for Trump by reminding them of his family’s historical surname, Drumpf—is still going too, and in a recent episode covered Trump’s efforts to reshape the Department of Justice. Jimmy Kimmel and Seth Meyers have consistently pursued Trump since his rise; even Jimmy Fallon, who was once reluctant to get political, will now occasionally take a shot. Some new impersonators have risen to take Baldwin’s place: James Austin Johnson and Shane Gillis have both played the president with some success in recent years. But in this cohort, things aren’t quite the same as they were. Johnson’s impression hasn’t created the kind of splash that Baldwin’s once did; his version of Trump is reminiscent of the de rigueur presidential impressions of SNL seasons past. (That’s no judgment of comedic value—Johnson’s version is as accurate as it gets.) And last year, when CBS decided to end its long-running program The Late Show With Stephen Colbert, late-night TV lost one of its most prominent and consistent critics. The administration’s attempts to chill dissenting speech elsewhere, although disturbing, don’t appear to have suppressed this sort of comedy’s prevalence in American culture. And in one case, such attempts had the opposite effect: In September, ABC suspended Kimmel’s show after Federal Communications Committee Chairman Brendan Carr demanded that it do so; a few days later, in response to a public outcry, the network reversed course. The fracas turned out to be good for ratings—Kimmel’s first show back was its most-watched in more than a decade. Read: Jimmy Kimmel ran right at his critics Still, the old guard doesn’t totally see the point. “All the majors that continue to just throttle him and carry on about him—move on to other people!” Baldwin told me. “It’s not gonna make any difference.” Atamanuik told me that by fixating on Trump, comedians risk missing the bigger issues, and that young people especially have “an anger at the entire system.” (The top-three late-night talk shows have been fighting for viewers in the coveted 18–49 age demographic for more than a decade.) “They know that Trump is just a product of something else,” he said. Meanwhile, Cooper said a few years ago that she has “no desire” to revisit her lip-syncs, and that “there’s nothing left to really do with it.” Brent Terhune, another veteran of the short-form-video scene that flourished during the coronavirus pandemic, is less gloomy about the future of Trump-inflected comedy. This is partially because he’s not interested in taking on the president directly. Instead, he makes videos in the persona of a Trump fan. The character he’s played for nearly a decade, “Alpha Male,” is a sort of disgruntled MAGA vlogger—“a reaction and a reflection of people that are Trump die-hards,” Terhune told me. Part of what distinguishes the performance is that it’s at least somewhat empathetic; he said he’s always being told that Alpha Male reminds people of their brothers, dads, and uncles. His view is that because the Alpha Male character isn’t just about skewering Trump, it could potentially outlast his administration. Terhune’s enthusiasm has certainly outlasted that of many of his peers, so maybe on-screen political comedy is just in need of new subjects. Although Atamanuik no longer consistently plays Trump, he has lately been impersonating Robert F. Kennedy Jr.—and Baldwin suggested that House Speaker Mike Johnson and former Acting Director of National Intelligence Bill Pulte could be deserving targets. Or maybe you can make the same joke only so many times before you start wondering what it is you’re really doing. In 1962, when the impressionist Vaughn Meader released his landmark comedy album, The First Family, which poked fun at the Kennedys, the anthropologist Margaret Mead remarked that its very existence illuminated something about American society. “This making fun of people in authority is very healthy,” she told Life magazine. “It is the difference between democracy and tyranny.” The impressionists I spoke with might hesitate to agree with that assessment. But beyond the easy laugh of a presidential impersonation, humor can still be a potent force in defending a free society against tyranny—that is, if comedians are willing to keep finding new angles rather than repeat old gags.

The Gun Lobby’s Most Cynical Move Yet
2026-08-24

The Gun Lobby’s Most Cynical Move Yet

The Atlantic | 2026-08-24

Visit Original Website
As a newspaper reporter, I covered my first school shooting in 2007 at Virginia Tech, where 32 students and faculty were murdered and 17 others were wounded. In one story, I recounted how Emily, a 19-year-old sophomore, was lying partially behind a desk on the floor of her French class while the shooter gunned down her teacher and nearly every student around her before shooting himself in the head. I can’t forget the image of Emily, shot twice herself, who pretended to be dead but surreptitiously grabbed a cellphone on the floor that another student had used to call 911 before he was shot. I listened many weeks later to a recording of the call. At first, she was too terrified to speak. All the police dispatcher could hear was the sound of her breath and the loud pop pop pop of gunfire all around her. “I can’t talk,” she whispered, gasping for breath. “Please hurry.” After the Virginia Tech massacre, I reported on many other school shootings, including Sandy Hook in 2012, when twenty 6- and 7-year-olds were shot to death in their first-grade classrooms and six staff members were killed. Attorney General Eric Holder, whom I covered at the time as The Washington Post’s Justice Department reporter, told me that walking through the bullet-riddled classrooms still caked with blood was the most difficult day of his professional life. He later cried openly when he saw the crime-scene photos of the children lying dead in their classrooms. I wrote about so many school massacres that I lost count. After each one, I reached out to the National Rifle Association, the National Shooting Sports Foundation (the gun industry’s trade association), and other organizations that the nation’s gun lobby comprises for their response. They rarely commented beyond a statement offering thoughts and prayers to the victims and their families. I twice attended the world’s largest firearms trade show, held annually in Las Vegas for tens of thousands of gun makers, sellers, and other professionals but closed to the general public. There, too, leaders of the NRA, other advocates, and representatives of the multibillion-dollar gun industry would not comment on school shootings, except to reiterate their support for armed guards in schools and a government registry of mentally ill people, and to resist calls to strengthen background checks and restrict access to semi-automatic rifles such as the AR-15. William H. Frist: The politics of gun safety are changing. I should know. But the gun lobby had another response to school shootings behind the scenes—one I didn’t know about at the time. A study by Stanford University researchers this year found that after fatal school shootings, pro-gun political-action committees dramatically increased their contributions to their preferred U.S. House candidates in the districts where the shootings occurred, when those races were competitive. These shifts in spending were not observed after other incidents of mass gun violence or after nonfatal school shootings—only when children were killed in schools. Over the many years that I reported on gun violence, the gun lobby’s enormous campaign spending was no secret. But the Stanford study reveals how these groups targeted their money, raising their aggressive tactics to a new level. In the first study of its kind, the authors analyzed 25 years of data, linking campaign-finance records to every fatal K–12 school shooting from 2000 to 2024. They then compared campaign contributions between the districts that did and did not have a school shooting. After school shootings where children died, pro-gun contributions to candidates in competitive congressional districts increased on average by more than 30 percent. And when the shooting occurred within two months of an election, contributions surged by 2,820 percent. “The data shows that the gun lobby saves its political firepower for the moments when public grief is greatest and lawmakers are under the most pressure to act,” Eric A. Baldwin, one of the authors, told me. In November 2021, for example, a 15-year-old killed four students and injured seven other people at Oxford High School, in Michigan. The pro-gun PACs contributed $50,650 during the 2021–22 election cycle in the district where the high school was located, a 434 percent increase from the previous cycle and a 591 percent increase from an average of the previous five cycles, according to the Stanford researchers. In December 2013, a 17-year-old senior was killed at Arapahoe High School, in a suburb of Denver. Before the shooting, the pro-gun PAC contribution had been $1,000. Afterward, it soared to $49,470. Gun-safety PACs also increased their spending in competitive districts after school shootings. But the pro-gun groups were by far the dominant spenders until 2018, the year of the Parkland shooting, which left 17 students and faculty dead at a high school in Florida; at that point, gun-safety groups began to significantly increase their spending, sometimes equaling or even surpassing the spending of the pro-gun groups. The Stanford researchers did not find that increased spending by either side after fatal school shootings measurably affected which party actually won a competitive House election. (The districts that the study looked at represent only a subset of the gun lobby’s overall election spending.) Nevertheless, the study shows how pro-gun PACs work to undermine anti-gun feelings in communities suffering from the trauma of children being killed in schools. In poll after poll for more than a decade, the majority of Americans have favored stricter gun laws. Most Americans specifically want a ban on weapons such as AR-15s, and also favor stricter background checks. That sentiment is particularly acute after a school shooting, when “public demand for reform is especially high,” according to the Stanford researchers. But, they write, the pro-gun spending surge means that “instead of yielding to constituent pressure, candidates are fortified to resist it and are empowered to reframe their opposition to reform as principled rather than unresponsive.” Tyler Austin Harper: ‘How much blood is your fun worth?’ Although the NRA’s power has been waning in recent years, it still spent $10 million in the 2024 election. Other pro-gun groups have emerged and are influencing national policy; together with the NRA, they spend about $14 million a year on lobbying. In response to lawsuits from a number of pro-gun entities, including Gun Owners of America, a federal judge in Texas recently found unconstitutional a 1934 federal law that placed strict requirements on anyone purchasing silencers, short-barreled rifles, and shotguns, among other firearms. The Justice Department did not immediately appeal the ruling. In recent months, the Trump administration has announced new actions to make it easier for gun purchasers to avoid background checks when obtaining firearms. In April, then–Acting Attorney General Todd Blanche and other Justice Department officials announced the rollback of gun regulations alongside leaders from the NRA, the National Shooting Sports Foundation, the National Association for Guns Rights, and Gun Owners of America. And House Republicans proposed a bill to cut nearly $300 million from the budget of the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms, and Explosives, the agency that enforces the nation’s gun laws. The killing of America’s children and teenagers in their classrooms has become normalized. From a young age, our children and grandchildren are being taught vocabulary and math alongside lessons in how to hide when an “active shooter” storms their classrooms. The Stanford study is a compelling reminder of how far the gun lobby will go to block any efforts to protect our children.

What It Takes to Reclaim Your Attention Span
2026-08-24

What It Takes to Reclaim Your Attention Span

The Atlantic | 2026-08-24

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Pocket Casts Of all the potential effects of using smartphones, the one that seems to inspire the most anxiety is what scrolling could be doing to people’s attention. On social media (ironically), many people express a desire to “heal” their attention spans from the damage they feel technology has done. They’re picking up on something real. In one 2025 study, researchers found that blocking the internet on people’s phones for two weeks led to improvements in sustained attention equivalent to reversing 10 years of cognitive decline. In this final episode of How to Touch Grass, host Julie Beck embarks on an experiment to see if she can heal her own attention span. Beck and co-host Natalie Brennan also hear from Jenny Odell, the author of How to Do Nothing and Saving Time, about how Odell’s antagonism for her phone actually helped her make peace with it, and how nature can teach us to pay deeper attention to the world around us. The following is a transcript of the episode: Julie Beck: Are you happy with your relationship with your phone, or are you not happy with it? Person 1: I wish I could reduce some of the Instagram Reel-ing, particularly at night, where I kind of go down the rabbit hole and it’s like, Oh my God, it’s midnight. Beck: Oh, yeah. I’ve been there. It’s like lost time. You know like when the aliens come and they get you, and then they put you back down, and it’s just lost time. Person 2: It has impacted my attention span. I get bored after half a paragraph of reading. Beck: Do you have a sense in your mind of when you noticed that you feel like your attention span was getting worse? Person 2: Probably been, like, since the start of COVID, probably in the last five years. Person 3: The thing that kills me sometimes is how much I want to look stuff up in conversations. Beck: And it’s always that word too, heal. It’s like we’re sick or we’re injured, and we must heal. Brennan: Is it something that you’ve been worried about? Beck: I do worry about my attention sometimes. Like every time I go to the gym lately, I forget to lock the locker that they have in the locker room. I put my stuff in there; I shut the door. Beck: That’s—I’m so happy to hear it. I can’t say the same. (Laughs.) Odell: I’m okay with my relationship with my phone and screen time. Beck: So can you walk me through a little bit? Odell: Yeah. I would say, like, part of the attention economy is the selling of quick fixes when, you know, habits form over time. They’re like grooves, right? Like, they need to be sort of formed over time. And so … Odell: Yes, exactly. And I think, you know, if you try to, say, go cold turkey, it can be frustrating when you don’t see immediate results. Odell: Or even within a couple of months, right? And then in that meantime, there’s all this uphill struggle of, like, your friends are still doing it. You need to be on Instagram to see this and that, and there’s just muscle-memory things, things you’re used to. You get up in the morning; you look at your phone. These are just—that’s hard. Like, those things are really ingrained, especially, I think, if you’re younger. I’m good now. But I just wanna stress that it took some time, and in that time there’s a little bit of a leap of faith. Beck: Yeah. I mean, I deleted TikTok off my phone for the third or fourth time, who’s counting? But like you were saying, the muscle memory of, like, the thumb going up and down, has just transferred to Instagram unfortunately. So we gotta work on that. Odell: I would say there’s, honestly, you know, I’m almost 40. Actually standing, like, with your very heavy head, like on your neck; when you get older, you start to notice the problems from that, and it’s like that is another kind of physical limit. I can’t sit at a computer for that long without injuring myself. And then I think I really had to think about my relationship to the news was a really big one that I hadn’t quite figured out. When you’re kind of scrolling, you're scrolling through it, you’re not really deeply engaging with any one particular issue. Odell: You’re just seeing a list of bad things, and they’re bad in all different kinds of ways, and they’re terrible. Like, even just sitting with one of those things produces this paralysis. It’s like, in order to do something, you need an orientation toward the future and a situation in which you feel that you have some agency. There are certain things that I have started to do in the last couple years. One of them is volunteering at a native-plant-restoration project every week. I’ve also been going to a lot of repair fairs. Also kind of volunteering-type things that are in person. Beck: Where you bring like a broken toaster or something— Odell: And learn how to fix it. Yeah. Totally. When I’m there, there is no desire to look at my phone. Like, it’s just so far from my mind, and it has this weird lasting effect throughout the week, actually, because I had this experience that was so direct, that was so meaningful. Beck: And tell me what is the good thing about the repair café, about the, you know, weeding the native plants? Like, what is the good thing? Odell: I think, you know, some of it is just recognition. I think we as humans, we want to be socially recognized, right? We want to feel seen. We wanna feel heard. We wanna feel welcomed. You know, it’s like Cheers or whatever. Odell: Like, you wanna walk into a room and have people be like, Hey, it’s you. I know you.I’m happy to see you. This is just kind of like a really basic thing that we want. I kinda think that social media, especially early on, the evil genius of it was that it was hijacking that. And so I feel like these are in-person events. I feel incredibly recognized and acknowledged. And then it’s like, when you feel This is the real thing, it becomes very obvious what is not the real thing. That’s a big part of it. And I think you’re there. You have a shared project. You’re getting some traction with something, right? Instead of just sort of constantly being washed over with information and not really having anything to grasp onto. Beck: For most people in this day and age, totally going without a smartphone just isn’t possible, or at least not practical. Like, the infrastructure of society, you know, they’ve got the QR-code menu at the restaurant, the stores are all accepting Apple Pay and Google Pay, and so many websites require you to receive a text and then put the code in to even get in there. So trying to resist getting sucked into your phone just feels like resisting the inevitable sometimes. Have you found a way to resolve this tension for yourself? Odell: I live in the same society you’re describing, and I just used my phone to pay for lunch before this. But then I put it away. Odell: I use the phrase standing apart as a way of kind of not leaving or believing in the fantasy of escape from something, and just sort of being like, I’m standing in a position from which I can see the thing. Beck: You’re not running away from it or— Odell: But I have critical distance. And I think about something like food. You know, you go to the grocery store and, I mean, depending on the grocery store you’re in, you kind of understand that 80 percent of the food in it is bad for you. We kinda know that, right? It has been that way long enough. Beck: Doesn’t stop me from getting the Flamin’ Hot Cheetos, but yes. I do know. Odell: (Laughs.) But it’s just this understanding that that space is just not set up for me to thrive. Odell: And I think that it’s helpful to maybe transpose that to your phone. What is a phone? A phone is a communication device. What I want from this is communication. The apps, you know, social media in particular, are not designed for you to thrive. They are designed for the opposite. (Laughs.) These are actively bad for me, and I won’t feel good. For me, it’s sort of a level of acceptance of, like, antagonism has been helpful. Where it’s like, I’m never going to have a peaceful relationship with that universe, and I’m just gonna kind of accept that and take what I need and leave. Beck: You know, recently I hear so many people, both online and in real life, talking about, “healing their attention spans” from the damage that they feel like technology has done. And I’m curious why, of all the things, you think people are worried about their attention specifically. Odell: One would hope that what you pay attention to is what is important to you. That’s an ideal situation. Odell: Right, exactly. So if you’re not paying attention to things that are important to you, there’s something, I think, like, deeply unsettling about that on the level of identity because it’s like, Am I even who I am? Right? Am I who I want to be if I’m paying my attention to something that I don’t feel I ever chose? Odell: I think it really deeply affects people on the level of, like, self-control and their sort of understanding of their identity. Beck: Yeah. Well, it seems like the solution that many people have landed on for healing their attention span is just trying to be online less, however they can. So do you think that logging off is the answer, or no? Odell: I think that the best approach, personally, is to just treat it as an experiment. Honestly, it makes it interesting, like I’m a curious person and I’m curious about myself and my own reaction to things. Kind of being like, Okay, I’m gonna try this. I’m gonna see what happens when I try this. Beck: Natalie, immediately after this interview, I really felt the itch to do what Jenny said—to try an experiment. And when I was thinking about how to do that, I remembered this study I had seen from 2025 where researchers blocked the internet on people’s smartphones for two weeks. Beck: And just doing that, they found that the improvement to people’' sustained attention was equivalent to reversing 10 years of cognitive decline. Beck: It’s huge. So I tried the experiment on myself for two weeks. Now, I didn’t perfectly replicate the conditions of the 2025 study, to be clear. They blocked mobile internet using an app called Freedom. I used, instead, this device called a Brick. Beck: Okay, so you know. So I used this device. I picked all the apps I was gonna block, and you have to physically touch your phone to the device to unlock it. Beck: I tried to, you know, keep all of the things that would allow me to communicate with other people, and so I blocked all games; I blocked all social media, email, Slack, internet browsers, and most internet-connected apps. Beck: So I blocked all of that for 23 hours a day. I was allowed one hour of non-Bricked time if I needed or wanted it. And I could do whatever I wanted on a computer, same as in the study. It was only the phone that was restricted. Brennan: And how were you going to test this? Beck: So I got in touch with someone from that 2025 study, and they shared an attention pre-test with me and a self-report questionnaire. So I took those for a baseline. Beck: I think he told me it’s actually, like, eight minutes. (Laughs.) I did both of those things before I started the experiment, and then I took them again at the end of the experiment. And I agreed to record a little audio diary along the way. Beck: Captain’s log, day one. My phone has been Bricked pretty much all day. I’ve definitely been kinda low energy. I don’t know if that’s, like, dopamine withdrawal. I’ve definitely been carrying my phone around with me everywhere even though there’s not really that much for me to look at. Morning, it’s day two. My husband is out of town right now, so it is daunting to have two of my biggest sources of entertainment, my husband and my phone, unavailable to me at this time, but we persevere. It is Saturday. It’s my first full weekend day. I ordered a big order of yarn, so I have a lot of knitting and crochet stuff to work on. Good morning, it’s day five. Knitting right now. Joe and I went birding. The one thing that I am noticing mood-wise is that I do feel pretty calm, like maybe more calm than usual. Brennan: OK, Julie! First check-in. What off the bat did you think you were going to do more of with your time that you didn’t wind up doing? Beck: Using the phone as a phone, as a communication device. Actually, like, calling my loved ones to catch up, you know, what the phone was invented for. Shout-out Alexander Graham Bell. But I think because I had blocked so much, and I was getting more used to just not having my phone on me and in my hand at all times, I was just thinking about my phone less overall, which was the goal.But I kinda come to think that as much as the distraction of the phone, like, has a cost, there’s also a cost to putting more distance there, because the phone is where the people are. Brennan: It’s interesting how much our relationship to our phones is intertwined with our relationship to others. Brennan: When we overuse our phones, that isn’t good for our relationships, and the way we use our phones is definitely influenced by other people. Odell: I think we don’t realize how much of our habits kind of live in other people. Like, think about it. When do you pick up your phone? It’s when the person you’re talking to picks up their phone, right? Like, the person picks up their phone; what are you gonna do? Sit there? Stare into space? That habit, like, lives between people. Beck: It’s so true, because my friends will, you know, text me TikToks still even though I am trying to get off TikTok. And then if I’m like, Okay, I’ll just watch the, like, Lord of the Rings meme that they sent me, of course my phone prompts me like, Don’t you wanna download TikTok again? And so it is, like, sort of driven by, like, the relationship of wanting to see this meme. But I guess my bigger question is: Is logging off to pay attention to something else in the real world actually better, or is it just different? Odell: I always think about, like, if you saw someone hiking and looking at their phone, you could be like, That’s so depressing. But then it’s like, what if they’re looking at iNaturalist? Odell: And they’re, like, identifying a plant. There are good things on the phone; there are bad things on the phone. There are good things IRL; there are bad things IRL. One thing that you can say is that the sort of quality of attention that one often experiences on the phone with things like social media is pretty narrow versus things off the phone. There are so many different kinds of experience, so many different scales of time, so many different scales of space. I love seeing movies in a movie theater. Odell: I’m like a movie-theater junkie. I love to see horror movies in a theater. Odell: So there’s things like that, but then there’s things like looking at a very small bird in your neighborhood, or being at a table with a group of people and being part of a conversation. Like, these are all just so different. Even if you think about your body in those spaces; it’s so different. I think it’s good to have a variety of different contexts for your attention.I think that’s probably healthy. Beck: We’re gonna take a quick break but when we come back: how the way we think about our phones affects the ways we use them. Odell: I think about it more as like: When I look at it as an object, it looks like a tool. And it did not look like a tool to me before. It looked like a black hole. Beck: Like a portal to the underworld or something. Brennan: Plus, did Julie fix her attention span? The results of her experiment, coming up. Beck: So day six, and wouldn’t you freaking know it, I did the exact same thing at the gym again. Sure enough, I forgot to lock it again. I’m legitimately concerned. Anyway, my attention is not fixed yet! Good morning. It’s day seven of the experiment. My average screen time is down 74 percent from last week. I certainly haven’t been, like a perfect screen-abstinent person this week. I’ve watched plenty of TV. I have been able to, I think, focus a bit better. I wonder how much of this could be achieved without such a draconian approach. I’ve been pretty surprised by how quickly I kind of, like, submitted. But yeah, just how quickly the cravings to check my phone all the time really receded. Brennan: Julie, day 7 was midway through your experiment. What started to shift for you by that point? Did the way you think about your phone start to change? Beck: I think what you learn, or at least what I learned when you block access to almost everything on your phone, is that the phone is not just one thing. There’s a lot of different stuff in there. Like— Beck: —you know? I think in our frustration with our attention span sometimes, people, myself included, will demonize the phone as a device. Like, smartphones are evil. Smartphones are sucking our life force, and they’re making us stupid, and that kind of flattens the problem, I think. What kept coming up for me is there’s a lot of different things I turn to my phone for, and some of them are more problematic than others. Like, checking the weather is, uh, very helpful. Brennan: You made a mistake in blocking that one (Laughs.) Beck: I did. Like, I realized that actually the first day. But I suppose that is the point of the experiment, right? To figure out which parts of my phone are actually adding to my life and which are taking away from it. Beck: Jenny, have you heard of these devices that block certain apps on your phone? There’s the Freedom app, the Unplug, the Brick device. Beck: What do you think of the recent popularity of these things? Odell: Honestly, I’m all for it. I don’t use any—I’m not trying to brag. I don’t need them. I think they’re great. There’s one in particular that I think is called SocialFocus, and it lets you kind of change the appearance of websites. Like, you could go to YouTube and not see the auto suggestions, for example. Odell: Or maybe not see comments. It kind of allows you to change what you see. And I think there was a Chrome extension that came out called News Feed Eradicator. And guess what happened? I stopped going to Facebook. And I didn’t decide to do that. It was because the News Feed is there to keep you on Facebook. So if you eradicate it, replace it with some sort of mindfulness quote, you go to the site; it’s inert. It can’t grab you in that way. Odell: And so I just simply stopped going. It was a totally unremarkable thing. I didn’t even tell anyone, Oh, I’m quitting Facebook. I just stopped going to it. And I think that’s really powerful. So that’s why I’m a big fan of anything that can give someone sort of granular control over what the—it sort of reintroduced choice in areas where people have never experienced choice. Because that, in and of itself, will cause someone to reflect upon what it is that they want. Beck: All of this reminds me of a study I’ve been thinking of a lot lately, that found using an app that was similar to the Brick for two weeks had such a big effect on people’s attention spans that it amounted to, like, reversing 10 years of cognitive decline— Odell: Wow. That’s incredible. I’m surprised and not surprised. Actually, honestly, this reminds me of, like, ecological restoration because when you restore a site, some things come back quickly and some things don’t, right? Like, you restore a wetland, some species are gonna come back almost immediately. The nutrient cycling of the entire site might take 100 years. Maybe our attention is similar in that, or our behavior, let’s say, it takes a long time to totally overhaul something. But at the same time, I think you might be surprised at how quickly certain things change. Like, in times when I was a little bit more embroiled online and using my phone a lot, and then I would maybe go somewhere, put it away for a while or whatever, I found that after a couple of days, I would notice that my attention felt different. Odell: And by attention felt different, I mean I would notice details or small differences in things. It’s almost like having a higher-resolution image or something. Beck: Are there other seemingly small changes to your routine that you feel like might have that kind of big cognitive benefit? Odell: You know, I think trying to replace habits with other habits—I meditate in the morning, only just for, like, 10 minutes. I kind of calm down to a level where then it’s not going to as easily occur to me to then pick up my phone, or at least I'll be a little bit more reflective about it. Beck: Yeah. My thing so far, my recent thing has been taking up knitting and crochet, and this seems so small, but it’s like now while I’m watching TV, instead of looking at my phone, my hands are in hand jail, right? Doing knitting. Beck: And it doesn’t seem so virtuous when I’m like, Now I’m only looking at one screen, but I feel like, you know, it’s an improvement. Beck: Maybe I'll get down to zero screens, TBD. But the other layer to that that I was just thinking about when it comes to phones and social media and stuff is, like, a lot of times where I’m going to learn how to do this new stitch or whatever is YouTube. Beck: So it’s not, you know, one is totally replacing the other, but there is sort of a relationship. Like now I am using that tool kind of for this other thing. Odell: Yeah. Right, and I think that points to an important distinction, which is maybe a bit more nuanced than just, say, screen time as a statistic. You know, you were asking earlier about my changed relationship to my phone. Odell: I think about it more as like: When I look at it as an object, it looks like a tool. And it did not look like a tool to me before. It looked like a black hole. Beck: Like a portal to the underworld or something. Odell: Yeah. Or, frankly, I wasn’t even looking at it. I’m already picking it up. Like, I’m already in there. I’m already in that world. There wasn’t even a point where I perceived the phone as an object. Odell: Yeah. And now I feel like the things that I use it for: the Merlin app, it’s like Shazam for bird songs, I use that. I use iNaturalist. I use the Constellation app, voice memos for, you know, recording birds. That relationship, to me, feels really different because the intention and the purpose, I feel like I’m in control of that. I mean on my phone, the first home screen is all what I would call observational apps, so camera, voice memo, iNaturalist, Merlin. Odell: I’m kind of trying to remind myself of how I wanna use the phone. Like, I now associate my phone with those activities. Beck: Being inspired by nature, how being in nature helps you, you know, pay attention. A common piece of advice for those who have been brain-rotted by the internet is to go touch grass. So are you also telling us to touch grass? Odell: Yeah, pretty much. And, I mean, the weeds that I weed at my weekly project are grass, so— Odell: —actually, I’m saying Kill grass. No, I’m just kidding. (Laughs.) But the reason I think it became such a—that phrase caught on was because it’s just so obviously true. The obvious truth of it is, like, why it’s funny and annoying at the same time. Beck: Yeah. What is it that you think the natural world has to teach us about attention? Odell: Complexity, basically. Like, I think it's a master class in complexity. It is kind of an antidote to the idea of the easy answer, which I think—I really blame Instagram for the oversimplification of political issues.Like, personally, that is just my ax to grind. And the idea that you could sort of absolve yourself by reposting something is just—yeah. It’s upsetting to me. But I think that there’s no easy answers to anything. Like, if you are a birder and you like birds, well, guess what? You’re gonna have to learn about trees. Odell: And then gonna have to learn about, like, seasons, and then, I don’t know, there’s just—everything is so tied to everything else, and there are so many things that are sort of, like, neither good nor bad or sort of arguable. We need lessons in that right now. Like, we need lessons in the sort of, like, Let me pick a point in space to look at, and then let me look at the context around that. Okay, now let me look at the context around that. And now you’ve sort of widened this whole ecosystem. I think the opposite of that is what happens online. Beck: So Jenny, if someone is just starting to rethink the relationship they have with their devices, what advice would you give them? Odell: Try to take an outsider’s perspective on yourself. I’m a big journaler, so I’ve journaled since I was a kid. So that would work for me. I would look at my phone, I would look at some stuff on my phone, and then I would just write down, kinda what it was and then how I felt about it. And I would be, like, brutally honest. Odell: Just pretend like you’re a scientist. And just talk to a friend about it. You could write it down, like, whatever. But, you know, I think there needs to be that, like, every so often you step to the side and you go, Okay, what did I just do? Like, Why did I do that? How did that make me feel? You can be super brief about it. Just do it for a week. Just see what it tells you. Odell: Before you try to make any changes, because the danger is that a person feels shame, they feel that it’s bad. They’re like, Okay, I need to change this, and then they try really hard to change it, and then it doesn’t go the way they wanted, and then they feel more shame. And now it’s just, like, a spiral. Beck: Yeah. I think especially with attention for whatever reason, it does feel very shameful, this like, Oh, I can’t focus; I can’t pay attention. I can’t even, like, read a book without having the desire to pick up my phone. people think it means that they’re a bad person, and it doesn’t. You just gotta be patient and try a bunch of different things and maybe it’ll be more interesting than you think. Beck: Guys, huge news. Huge news. I locked my gym locker today. Nature is healing. I am the virus. For once in my goddamn life, I locked my gym locker. It is day 13. I was on the computer all day for work because computer is where the money comes from. You can still screen fatigue yourself. (Laughs.) The old-fashioned way. Captain’s log, the final full day of the experiment. Over the past two weeks, I read one full novel, I started two other books, I finished an audiobook, I crocheted a hat, I watched all of the first season of The Traitors. It’s not like I, you know, found myself with a magical abundance of time. I think it’s more like I just paid more attention and spent more time doing the things that I already like to do. That was time mostly well spent, so attention mostly well spent. Brennan: Julie, when you set out to go on this experiment you got in touch with one of the researchers from the 2025 study, Kostadin Kushlev from Georgetown University, who agreed to go over the results of your post-experiment attention test with us. And before we call him up, are you nervous about your results, not so nervous about these results? Will it mean less to you if your attention span didn’t change on paper? Beck: I mean, I guess I’m a little nervous. I mean, look, I am a nerd and I always wanna get an A+ on my projects. But I really do feel better, and if it’s just a placebo effect, well, a placebo effect is still an effect, right? Like, I’ll take it. Brennan: Hi, I’m Natalie. I'm Julie’s producer. I’m also gonna be sitting in on this call. Beck: Hey, Kosta. It’s Julie. How are you? Beck: I’m doing OK! So, can we just dive right in? Beck: And tell me, before I started the experiment, how was my baseline attention span? Kushlev: So your score was about three, 3.02. Beck: Is that good? Good for a 35-year-old? Good, bad? Kushlev: That’s pretty good. Essentially, you did about how our participants did. It’s a little closer to the level you might see in young adulthood or for somebody in their 60s. (Laughs.) Beck: (Laughs.) Okay. Humbling. Humbling. Kushlev: Yes. Yes. But, you know, but once we get to your post score— Kushlev: So your post score was actually hugely better. So it was 3.8. And that is much bigger improvement than we saw on average in our study. So your improvement is about four to five times bigger than what the average participant in our study gained. Beck: Write it down, Natalie. Write it down. (Laughs.) Kushlev: Yeah. So you did really well on that. Beck: Amazing, I think. Is 3.1 to 3.8 good? Have I reversed my cognitive decline? Or how would you characterize that? Kushlev: You’re basically off the charts. Kushlev: But it might take a while to reap the benefits of this improved attention. Beck: Right. And it’ll probably go away if I just start scrolling Instagram again. Kushlev: It’s certainly not a permanent increase. But I do like to point out that, you know, just taking a two-week break and not even a complete break from your phone, just sort of like reducing your use, can actually improve that ability to sustain attention. Our lives are just like so tied to these devices, so sometimes the question might be: Have we like essentially permanently damaged our, you know— Kushlev: —brains? Like, have we changed our neurobiology and so forth? And so this seems to suggest that no, there is a way out. So it might be worth doing some of those digital-detox interventions. Beck: Thank you so much, Kosta. It was great chatting with you! Beck: Unbearably smug. No, no, no. I’m just kidding. No, I feel relieved, honestly. Like, even if the true effect size isn’t as big as it seemed, ’cause I am just one person; it’s not, you know, a full scientific study— Beck: —it still feels really good to know that it’s possible to improve so quickly. Beck: So it’s actually kind of wild that you can do something as simple as this and, like, bounce back so quickly. Brennan: Are you going to keep using the Brick? Like, how are you feeling about—where’s your relationship at between you and your phone right now? Beck: My relationship with my phone is cordial. We’re not—it’s not warm— Beck: —but it’s not cold. We’re cordial. We’re friendly acquaintances. Brennan: That sounds like how people describe their exes. Beck: Yeah. And, well, we haven’t broken up. It really is—it’s a situationship— Beck: —that is ongoing forever. No, I’m definitely gonna continue using the Brick, and I have been still. Twenty-three hours a day? You know, maybe not. Beck: I have been Bricking my phone, at a minimum, before bed, so I can't doom scroll right before I sleep, and I can’t look at my emails or Instagram the second that I regain consciousness. So that does help. But I would say that throughout this, I kept thinking about my favorite poet, Mary Oliver, and one thing that she wrote was, “To pay attention, this is our endless and proper work.” Beck: So this season, I think what I’ve learned is: Your attention is your life. And I think on some level I knew that already, but after this experiment, I’ve really been feeling it. Brennan: We started this season because we wanted to understand why so many people were feeling the pull to log off, right? We’ve explored a lot of the ways in which the internet can pull you into thoughtless patterns, whether it’s the way Instagram makes us feel bad about our bodies or the way that AI chatbots can send us into an anxious spiral. But it seems like what we are maybe actually looking for, right now, is ways to be more thoughtful. Beck: Yeah, I wanted to feel more thoughtful about my life. I wanted to not to feel as much like I’m literally giving my life away to things that I don’t even care about. I wanted to stop nickel-and-diming the moments of my life away. Brennan: Being intentional about where you give your attention. Beck: That’s all for this season of How to Touch Grass. Tallying
2026-08-23

Tallying

The Atlantic | 2026-08-23

Visit Original Website
I tally the dead. I tally today’s dead. I seek a tally of the dead or seek yesterday’s dead. I try to count them, the dead. Reports tell me there are, today, more dead. I announce the dead—the house is quiet. I try to count. I seek numbers. I provide numbers. I wake trying. I cover myself in numbers, trying to keep still. I seek some stillness beyond the dead—I try to just manage. I tally the dead, each pair of lungs, I wander through numbers, the daffodils nevertheless fly up. I hide the numbers, paper over what I know. The warm house fills.   What are you doing, he says. I say prayers for, try to put faces to the dead as if to anchor the dead—I condemn the dead, their utter defenselessness.   Again I try to spare no numbers, the ones who have died, not the ones about to die, and when I wake they are there, counted and uncounted.   When life takes more away, I keep the dead inside me, the ones not injured but gone, although many are injured. My counting— what even matters? I don’t feel like going out. Soon I am counting. At the gate of the house I feel the faint dusk resolve. I bless the dead, I forget the dead from last year, I lose more dead, I tally what I can.   I count through the morning. Light drifts across the thick rug. I move my hands as if I am counting the dead. As if I am altering the dead in my hands as I count.
The Atlantic
An American City, With Troops on Its Streets
2026-08-23

An American City, With Troops on Its Streets

The Atlantic | 2026-08-23

Visit Original Website
The sight of military troops patrolling in a peaceful American city would once have been strange, if not outright alarming. But since last August, when Donald Trump first deployed the National Guard to Washington, D.C., residents have become accustomed to seeing men and women in camouflage military fatigues on duty throughout the city and its public-transit system. Sometimes, they trail federal officers from agencies such as the FBI and U.S. Marshals Service, dispatched by Trump to patrol the district in bulletproof vests. More often, the troops wander around unaccompanied, in small groups. D.C. has now entered its second year with federal troops on its streets. President Trump initially ordered the Guard into the capital city, sent federal agents to work as beat cops, and briefly seized control of D.C.’s local police department following the attempted carjacking of a former member of Elon Musk’s DOGE team. The early days of the D.C. Safe and Beautiful Task Force produced dramatic headlines. As the year dragged on, though, its activities faded from national news coverage, even as armed troops and federal agents continued to patrol. The Pentagon says it plans to keep the Guard in D.C. through January 2029—which will cost taxpayers more than $1 billion. For all that money, the Guard’s purpose here is largely inscrutable. “They don’t bother nobody; they just go back and forth, and back and forth,” Aundrea’ Pulliam, a Washington Metropolitan Area Transit Authority employee at the Dupont Circle Metro stop, told us. (As we spoke, a few Guard members walked by, heading toward the escalators.) Occasionally, Pulliam said, she has seen Guards help a rider who’s “super drunk,” or check out an abandoned backpack. But most of the time, a station manager, Deidra Foster, told us, it seems like “they’re really bored.” Whereas other American cities targeted by Trump with National Guard deployments found success in court, D.C.’s lack of statehood limits its ability to fight back. The presence of the task force represents a particularly Trump-era indignity for the city: both comic and menacing; abusive and ineffective; appalling to some and, as time goes on, exhaustingly commonplace. Antidemocratic though the federal takeover may be, D.C. residents have been forced to live with it. At first, the 800 or so troops called up were locals: members of the D.C. National Guard, which, unlike other state and territorial Guard units, is under the president’s direct control. Then, governors from around the country, mostly Republicans, began volunteering their own troops. The total head count was about 2,000 before ballooning to 5,000 before the America 250 celebrations—enough that, in some neighborhoods, walking to the Metro or picking up groceries without encountering Guardsmen became nearly impossible. The Trump administration has promoted the Guard’s presence as an overwhelming success. “Washington D.C. went from High Crime to NO CRIME — Thank you President Trump!” the president posted on Truth Social in early July. (This month, Trump claimed that crime in the city had dropped by 92 percent, citing a favorite number of his.) True, crime has largely fallen in the capital over the past year—but it was on the decline before Trump’s surge, and cities across the country without any comparable federal presence have seen similar positive trends. (Numbers reported by D.C.’s Metropolitan Police Department suggest that violent crime in the district may, in fact, be slightly up.) A recent report by the moderate Niskanen Center found that deployment of the National Guard led to a roughly 25 percent decrease in petty property crime over the first six months, but had “no measurable effect on violent crime.” Another analysis, published by the left-leaning Center for American Progress, reported “strong evidence that the deployment of the National Guard did not result in a measurable decrease in violent crime rates.” In response, Trump threatened to sue the organization. The National Guard may not have done much in part because there isn’t much for them to do. Troops have been sworn in as deputy U.S. marshals, which gives them some powers of law enforcement, but the terms of their appointment state that they cannot independently conduct searches or make arrests. (They can, however, detain suspected lawbreakers until either local or federal law enforcement arrives.) According to a recent analysis of court records by Reuters, troops have been mentioned in only 1.3 percent of criminal cases prosecuted in D.C. Superior Court over the past year—and in some of those cases, they were the victims. The media-relations page for the Guard’s D.C. presence includes stories about picking up trash and reuniting a lost boy with his parents on the National Mall. Perhaps most notable, the Guard has begun carrying the overdose-prevention drug naloxone, commonly known by its brand name, Narcan. Early this month, the D.C. National Guard’s interim commanding general told reporters that Guardsmen had administered the drug 325 times. Sergeant Hare, a Guardsman outside the Woodley Park Metro station, who declined to give his first name, citing National Guard policies, told us that he had lost track of how many drug-related emergencies he’d helped with, but said it was “the main thing” he’d done during his deployment. “Could this be handled without having the National Guard in D.C.? Probably,” Regina LaBelle, an addiction-policy professor at Georgetown University, told us. But “since they are here,” she said, “it’s really good that they are using naloxone where it’s needed.” Whatever the troops are or aren’t doing, their presence is a reminder of Trump’s power to shape the world as he alone pleases. America’s Founders understood domestic deployment of the military as the tool of a tyrant, and put in place legal protections against it—protections that Trump has now set about eroding. The president treats America’s cities as though they’re populated by scofflaws to be subjugated rather than by citizens with a say in their own future, and deploying the Guard to D.C. is a way to make that vision into a reality. “They’re an armed occupying force in our city,” Merawi Gerima, an organizer with the D.C. Alliance Against Racist and Political Repression, told us. Gerima and other activists we spoke with emphasized that they understood the Guard’s deployment as only one aspect of the larger federal presence in D.C. As part of the D.C. Safe and Beautiful Task Force, federal agents have been reassigned from their normal responsibilities to act as beat cops, dispatched from agencies such as the FBI; the Drug Enforcement Administration; the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives; Customs and Border Protection; the Secret Service; the Park Police; the U.S. Marshals Service; the Diplomatic Security Service; and, oddly, the IRS. Although Guard troops are the visual signature of Trump’s seizure of control over the city, they comprise the least energetic component of the task force. The federal-law-enforcement officers deployed across D.C. at Trump’s directive have been far busier, and more aggressive. Twice this past fall, agents with ICE’s Homeland Security Investigations division fired their weapons during traffic stops. Months later, a deputy U.S. marshal shot and killed a robbery suspect. After the administration lifted restrictions on car chases by Park Police, the agency launched a flurry of high-speed chases; multiple crashes and at least two deaths—of a bystander and a fleeing driver—soon followed. During the first six months of the surge, immigration enforcement and other federal agents roamed the city, detaining residents off the street. ICE has been less prominent since December, when a D.C. federal court enjoined the government from carrying out mass immigration arrests without a warrant. But Sandra Benavente, the advocacy manager at the immigrant-aid nonprofit Ayuda, told us that immigrant communities still feel the aftershocks of those arrests. “We’ve seen since last summer this climate of fear around all uniformed law enforcement,” she said—including the National Guard. Some of the group’s clients, including those relying on Ayuda for food or assistance with seeking legal status, have missed appointments because they are afraid of being stopped on their way downtown. Although ICE activity remains limited in D.C., other federal agencies continue to police the city in unprecedented numbers, sometimes alongside officers with D.C.’s local Metropolitan Police Department. The “lockup list” in D.C. Superior Court, which records people detained overnight by law enforcement for arraignment the next business day, routinely hovers near 100—far greater than the pre-surge norm of 60 or so. “There’s a difference in the intensity of the policing,” Gerima said, particularly “in Black and brown neighborhoods.” (All three of the men shot at by task-force members, including the person who died, were Black.) The additional federal presence provides law enforcement with more resources, he said, “and what they’re using that to do is to grab anybody for anything, any semblance of a crime.” Minor violations of local laws that might previously have brought about nothing more than a ticket—such as drinking from an open container—are now resulting in arrests by federal agents. Early in the National Guard’s D.C. deployment, especially after Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth ordered that troops begin carrying firearms, some residents worried about potential violence. So far, the troops deployed to the capital have not been responsible for any civilian deaths—the bare minimum of what one might hope for from a military deployment on U.S. soil. In fact, the only bloodshed related to the Guard involved troops as victims, when an Afghan man who had served with U.S. Special Forces in Afghanistan shot two members of the West Virginia Guard near the White House, one of whom later died. Apart from this burst of violence, the troops’ stay in the capital has thus far been largely peaceful, albeit the ominous sort of peace that results from uniformed men and women strolling around with guns. In one notable exception partially caught on video, three troops were physically aggressive in detaining a woman outside her home—pushing her to the ground and zip-tying her hands so tightly that, she later wrote in a claim against the Guard, the plastic cuffs “ruptured one or more ligaments in her wrist,” requiring surgery. The National Guard says that the woman, Anna King, had thrown “unknown liquid” at other Guard members. Prosecutors brought assault charges against King for the liquid incident and for allegedly harming a member of the Guard while being detained; she has pleaded not guilty. Trump’s America is a land of perpetual indignation, where scandals that might once have ended a presidency have become commonplace. After a year of federal presence, D.C. is suspended somewhere between the routine and the outrageous. Still, activists we spoke with described continued energy to fight Trump’s overreach. Some organize protests; others quietly keep tabs on federal agents in their neighborhoods. The protest group Free D.C., which has grown prominent over the past year, encourages residents to voice their complaints by writing to governors who have sent their National Guard troops to the city. “I feel like it’s just better for me to ignore them,” Jill Robinson, a health educator who has lived in the area for four decades, told us. Yet “I feel angry and annoyed.” Robinson was attending a Free D.C. event, one of nearly a dozen planned by the group in the days leading up to August 11, the first anniversary of the National Guard deployment. She had attended previous Free D.C. training sessions, including on how to stay safe if law enforcement becomes aggressive at a protest. This event was meant to honor Nolberto Meza, the bystander who was struck and killed in Northwest D.C. during a Park Police chase of a suspected stolen car. (Meza had arrived in the U.S. from Venezuela, seeking asylum.) Amid torrents of summer rain, several dozen people had gathered on the bridge where Meza was hit, wearing ponchos, clutching umbrellas, and carrying signs with slogans such as END THE OCCUPATION. The attendees included more seasoned protesters like Robinson, but also newcomers such as Ella and Ant Peels, who’d moved to Washington in May from Annapolis, Maryland. They’d read about the deployment before their move, but seeing the Guardsmen out and about—“maybe three times every time we take a walk”—had made them uncomfortable. “It definitely gives an eerie feeling to living here,” Ella said. “It doesn’t make me feel safe.” Those opposed to the increased federal presence have notched some wins. U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro’s office has been continually stymied in federal court by opposition from D.C. residents sitting on grand and petit juries. This spring, during D.C.’s Democratic primary, the mayoral candidate Janeese Lewis George won a decisive victory after advocating for a more adversarial approach to the president than that of the current mayor, Muriel Bowser. The D.C. city council passed emergency measures requiring the MPD to keep records of federal officers’ role in arrests and incidents involving the use of force. But the city’s status as a federal enclave starkly restricts its ability to respond to Trump. Last month, all 13 members of the council signed letters calling on governors to withdraw troops from the city. A top Justice Department official dismissed the letters as “impotent theatrics.” (In response to a legal challenge filed by D.C. Attorney General Brian Schwalb, a federal judge ruled that both the D.C. and out-of-state Guard deployments were illegal, but that ruling has been paused while an appeals court considers the question.) The council can push MPD for transparency, but it has little power to constrain federal law enforcement, especially if those agents are patrolling separately from local police. These limitations underline the authoritarian tilt of the federal presence in D.C. The troops and federal agents are here without the city’s say, at the pleasure of one man only, and there is little the city can do about it. The legal restraints on D.C. are unique, but the city is not going through this experience entirely alone. To the southwest, Memphis has a similar federal presence, alongside a deployment of hundreds of Tennessee National Guard troops sent by the state’s Republican governor. Trump announced the Memphis Safe Task Force a month after the D.C. task force began its surge, touting Memphis as a “replica of our extraordinarily successful efforts” in D.C. Now both the Guard and a contingent of federal agents have patrolled Memphis for almost a year. In four separate incidents, city residents died after being shot by members of the task force: Two National Guard troops were responsible for one shooting; the agents in the other three incidents were with the DEA and ICE’s Homeland Security Investigations division. (In one incident, it remains unclear whether the person died of the gunshot wound or a self-inflicted stabbing.) Memphis, a red-state “blue dot” whose autonomy has been continually rolled back by Republican state legislators, mirrors D.C.’s vulnerability. In both Memphis and D.C., people harmed by the task force have limited options for legal redress. Although federal civil-rights law allows lawsuits against state and local officials for violations of constitutional rights, options for suing federal officers are far more limited. In one case, the ACLU is pushing for accountability after MPD, called in by an Ohio National Guard sergeant, handcuffed and detained a D.C. resident who had been protesting the Guard by trailing troops while blasting “The Imperial March,” from Star Wars, on his phone. D.C. settled, but litigation continues against the sergeant, who argues that he was acting in a federal role. The activists we spoke with said they see their push to rid D.C. of this military presence as part of a larger effort to oppose authoritarian overreach. The “Imperial March” case makes the metaphor for them. It is absurd—a Star Wars joke that ended with someone in handcuffs—but also unnerving. In a city, or country, with fewer and fewer checks on presidential authority, a small act of dissent can provoke a big abuse of power.
